The **nuclear program of Iran** consists of an extensive infrastructure of research sites, [uranium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uranium "Uranium") mines, [research reactors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Research%5Freactor "Research reactor"), uranium processing facilities, [enrichment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enriched%5Furanium "Enriched uranium") sites, the [Bushehr Nuclear Power Plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bushehr%5FNuclear%5FPower%5FPlant "Bushehr Nuclear Power Plant") (the country’s sole operational power reactor, currently expanding with two additional units), and the developing [Darkhovin Nuclear Power Plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darkhovin%5FNuclear%5FPower%5FPlant "Darkhovin Nuclear Power Plant") in [Khuzestan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khuzestan%5Fprovince "Khuzestan province"). The program’s research capabilities are anchored by several facilities, most notably the [Tehran Research Reactor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tehran%5FResearch%5FReactor "Tehran Research Reactor") and the [heavy water reactor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heavy-water%5Freactor "Heavy-water reactor") at [Khondab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khondab%5FHeavy%5FWater%5FResearch%5FReactor "Khondab Heavy Water Research Reactor"). Iran's uranium processing infrastructure is centered in [Isfahan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isfahan%5Fprovince "Isfahan province"), which serves as a hub for conversion and fuel fabrication, while primary enrichment activities are conducted at [Natanz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natanz%5FNuclear%5FFacility "Natanz Nuclear Facility"), [Fordow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fordow%5FUranium%5FEnrichment%5FPlant "Fordow Uranium Enrichment Plant"), and within the [Isfahan complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isfahan%5FNuclear%5FTechnology/Research%5FCenter "Isfahan Nuclear Technology/Research Center"). 

Iran’s stated objective is a self-sufficient nuclear energy cycle to diversify its power grid and produce [medical radioisotopes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radiopharmacology "Radiopharmacology"), citing a legal right to peaceful technology under the [Non-Proliferation Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty%5Fon%5Fthe%5FNon-Proliferation%5Fof%5FNuclear%5FWeapons "Treaty on the Non-Proliferation of Nuclear Weapons") (NPT). While viewed as a cornerstone of Iranian [technological nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Techno-nationalism "Techno-nationalism"), the program has been the subject of a decades-long diplomatic and military standoff, particularly with the [United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United%5FStates "United States") and [Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel "Israel"), culminating in large-scale conflicts in [2025](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelve-Day%5FWar "Twelve-Day War") and [2026](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026%5FIran%5Fwar "2026 Iran war").

The program began in 1957 as part of the US-led [Atoms for Peace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atoms%5Ffor%5FPeace "Atoms for Peace") initiative and expanded significantly during the 1970s under the [Pahlavi era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pahlavi%5FIran "Pahlavi Iran"), which saw the start of the Bushehr project with Western assistance. Following the [1979 Iranian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian%5FRevolution "Iranian Revolution"), the program was initially deprioritized but was revived in the late 1980s.[\[4\]](#cite%5Fnote-4)[\[5\]](#cite%5Fnote-5)

During the 1990s and early 2000s, Iran developed several undeclared sites, which were identified by international observers in 2002 and 2009\. Following the collapse of negotiations with the [EU-3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EU-3 "EU-3") in 2005, the [International Atomic Energy Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International%5FAtomic%5FEnergy%5FAgency "International Atomic Energy Agency") (IAEA) Board of Governors found Iran in non-compliance with its NPT safeguards agreement,[\[6\]](#cite%5Fnote-IAEAIran2005-6)[\[7\]](#cite%5Fnote-7)[\[8\]](#cite%5Fnote-8) leading the [United Nations Security Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United%5FNations%5FSecurity%5FCouncil "United Nations Security Council") (UNSC) to pass a series of resolutions between 2006 and 2010 demanding a suspension of Iran's enrichment-related activities and targeting Iran’s banking, trade, and energy sectors.[\[9\]](#cite%5Fnote-9)[\[10\]](#cite%5Fnote-UNNews2-10)[\[11\]](#cite%5Fnote-IranSanction2-11)[\[12\]](#cite%5Fnote-12)[\[13\]](#cite%5Fnote-13) While Western intelligence agencies assessed that a dedicated weaponization project, the [AMAD Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AMAD%5FProject "AMAD Project"), was [discontinued in 2003](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatwa%5Fagainst%5Fnuclear%5Fweapons "Fatwa against nuclear weapons"), Iran's ongoing enrichment activities led to a decades-long cycle of [international sanctions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International%5Fsanctions%5Fagainst%5FIran "International sanctions against Iran") and a sustained campaign of covert sabotage, including the 2009 [Stuxnet cyberattack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation%5FOlympic%5FGames "Operation Olympic Games"), multiple [assassinations of Iranian nuclear scientists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassinations%5Fof%5FIranian%5Fnuclear%5Fscientists "Assassinations of Iranian nuclear scientists"), and strikes on facilities in [2020](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020%5FIran%5Fexplosions "2020 Iran explosions") and [2021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021%5FNatanz%5Fincident "2021 Natanz incident"), which Iran characterized as "nuclear terrorism."

In 2015, the [Joint Comprehensive Plan of Action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%5Fnuclear%5Fdeal "Iran nuclear deal") (JCPOA) was signed by Iran, [China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China "China"), [France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/France "France"), [Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russia "Russia"), the [United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United%5FKingdom "United Kingdom"), the US, and [Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germany "Germany"), under which Iran agreed to strict enrichment limits and extensive international monitoring at its nuclear facilities in exchange for sanctions relief. Although the JCPOA briefly stabilized the situation, this period of oversight ended in 2018 following the unilateral [withdrawal of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United%5FStates%5Fwithdrawal%5Ffrom%5Fthe%5FIran%5Fnuclear%5Fdeal "United States withdrawal from the Iran nuclear deal") and the subsequent ["Maximum Pressure"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maximum%5Fpressure%5Fcampaign "Maximum pressure campaign") campaign. In response to the US withdrawal, and citing ongoing acts of US and Israeli sabotage, Iran incrementally reduced its compliance and raised enrichment levels to 60%,[\[a\]](#cite%5Fnote-HEU-14) a move the Iranian government characterized as diplomatic leverage to secure the removal of reinstated sanctions.[\[14\]](#cite%5Fnote-Byman-15)[\[15\]](#cite%5Fnote-sweeping-16)[\[16\]](#cite%5Fnote-know-17)[\[17\]](#cite%5Fnote-happens-18) In August 2025, France, Germany, and the UK triggered the [snapback mechanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snapback%5Fmechanism%5Fof%5Fsanctions%5Fagainst%5FIran "Snapback mechanism of sanctions against Iran"),[\[18\]](#cite%5Fnote-:42-19) reinstating UN sanctions; that October, Iran, Russia, and China declared they viewed JCPOA terminated and the UN sanctions legally void.[\[19\]](#cite%5Fnote-20)

The standoff escalated into direct military conflict with the [Twelve-Day War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelve-Day%5FWar "Twelve-Day War") in June 2025 and a larger [joint U.S.-Israeli campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026%5FIran%5Fwar "2026 Iran war") in February 2026 targeting nuclear and ballistic missile infrastructure. These strikes, which included the assassination of high-ranking Iranian officials, were justified by the U.S. and Israel as preemptive measures to prevent a [nuclear breakout](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear%5Fbreakout%5Fcapability "Nuclear breakout capability").[\[20\]](#cite%5Fnote-21) Following a Pakistan-mediated [ceasefire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026%5FIran%5Fwar%5Fceasefire "2026 Iran war ceasefire") in April 2026, the parties entered [high-level negotiations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamabad%5FTalks "Islamabad Talks") in [Islamabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamabad "Islamabad"). However, a lasting settlement remains elusive; while the U.S. has demanded a permanent end to all uranium enrichment, Iran maintains its legal right to a civilian program. Analysts remain divided on the long-term impact of the conflict, debating whether the physical damage has permanently delayed the program or instead incentivized a transition from [nuclear latency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear%5Flatency "Nuclear latency") to the pursuit of a [completed deterrent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear%5Fweapon "Nuclear weapon").[\[21\]](#cite%5Fnote-The%5FGuardian%5FAttacking%5FIran%5FIncentivizes%5FWeaponizing-22)

## History

| [![icon](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/60px-Question_book-new.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Question%5Fbook-new.svg) | This section **relies on a [single source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Articles%5Fwith%5Fa%5Fsingle%5Fsource "Wikipedia:Articles with a single source")**.
